The Lambda stays the single validator because (a) Requirement 4.7's `authentication_failed` audit needs claim-level failures to reach code that can write it, (b) `token_use`, scope and the six-URL audience rule live in one in-process-testable place, and (c) a gateway JWT authorizer could not emit a challenge of its own either (H4). The verifier performs every check a gateway authorizer would (`kid`/signature, `iss`, `exp`, `aud`/`client_id`, scope) plus `token_use`. A separate API leaves the existing REST API — whose nested `ApiGatewayStack` once hit the 500-resource limit and now adds route groups as further nested stacks by convention (review-5 N2) — untouched (C7) and gets its own throttling budget, which bounds unauthenticated Lambda invocations |
| D3 | Token type and audience (Decision point 2, F2) | MCP clients present Cognito **access tokens** issued to a new app client `dda-portal-mcp-client` (public; `ExplicitAuthFlows = ['ALLOW_REFRESH_TOKEN_AUTH']` **only** — the hosted-UI authorization-code + PKCE grant and the refresh grant are the sole ways to obtain a token; no SRP, password or custom flow — review-3 H1) with scope `<canonical-url>/portal.tools` from a new resource server whose identifier **is the canonical MCP URL**; clients send RFC 8707 `resource=<endpoint URL>` so Cognito stamps `aud` (documented to work for "a URL of your choosing", so the view URLs need no extra resource servers). The server accepts a token iff signature/`iss`/`exp` verify, `token_use == 'access'`, `client_id` is the MCP app client, scope contains `<canonical>/portal.tools`, and `aud` (when present) is one of the deployable's six endpoint URLs; a token without `aud` is accepted on the strength of `client_id` + scope (documented deviation, see Security) | ID tokens are what the web UI uses; the REST API's Cognito authorizer (no scopes) accepts only ID tokens, so an MCP token can never be replayed against the REST API and a web ID token can never reach MCP — token passthrough is structurally impossible. Cognito's resource binding gives real RFC 8707 audience validation; the `client_id`+scope fallback covers clients that do not send `resource` without weakening the binding (only the MCP client can be granted the scope) |
| D4 | How tools reach portal logic | **Lambda-to-Lambda dispatch**: the MCP Lambda builds an API-Gateway-REST-proxy-shaped event for the backing route (method, `resource`, `path`, `pathParameters`, `queryStringParameters`, `body`) with `requestContext.authorizer.claims` = the verified token payload **verbatim**, `requestContext.identity` = the MCP client's IP/User-Agent, and a non-spoofable `event['mcp']` marker block, then `lambda:InvokeFunction` (RequestResponse) on the existing handler function. Requirement 3.9 interpretation: see Security | The handler's own routing, validation, RBAC, audit-before-effect, and job semantics run unmodified (1.3, 5.7, C4, C5). HTTP forwarding is impossible (REST routes need ID tokens) and forbidden (token passthrough). Importing handler modules in-process would need the union of 30 handlers' env vars, layers and IAM. The access token never leaves the MCP Lambda; handlers receive claims, not credentials |
| D5 | Pre-dispatch RBAC | The catalog declares `permissions` + `scope` per tool; the MCP Lambda evaluates them with `shared_utils.rbac_manager.has_permission(sub, usecase_id, permission, user_info=user)` **before** invoking; denial → `unauthorized_access` audit entry with the marker and a `not_authorized` tool error, nothing invoked; `RegistryUnavailable` → `authorization_unavailable` entry and an `authorization_unavailable` error. The handler's own check still runs after dispatch (defense in depth) | Requirement 3.1 asks for exactly this; it also gives marked denial entries for the many handlers that answer bare 403s via `check_user_access` (3.3) and guarantees "no part of the operation" runs (3.3) |
| D6 | MCP_Audit_Marker carrier | New optional top-level audit attributes `channel` (`'mcp'`; `'api'` on every other entry written after this change), `mcp_tool`, `mcp_server`, `mcp_client`, `mcp_request_id`. They are produced (a) by `shared_utils.attribution_from` from `event['mcp']` whenever a handler passes `identity=`/`event=`, and (b) by the **request audit context** (D25) for every `log_audit_event`/`record_audit_event_strict` call that passes neither — so **every** audit entry a backing handler writes during an MCP-originated invocation is marked, with no per-handler edit. The MCP Lambda writes an envelope entry `mcp_tool_call` only when the backing route writes no entry of its own on success (`handler_audits=False`) or when no handler ran | `event['mcp']` is a top-level key API Gateway never emits, so only the Lambda invoker can set it — a browser cannot forge the marker. The survey above shows most backing handlers pass neither `identity` nor `event`, so without D25 those entries would stay unmarked and MCP activity would be indistinguishable from browser activity (Requirement 4.3; H6) |
| D7 | Read-only tool auditing (F5) | Every Read_Only_Tool completion yields one marked entry (the handler's own when it writes one, else the `mcp_tool_call` envelope) in the existing table; no sampling, no CloudWatch-only mode | Requirement 4.1 is a SHALL; cost is negligible (DynamoDB on-demand write request units in us-east-1 cost US$0.625 per million — aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/pricing/on-demand/, fetched 2026-10-19, Research baseline "AWS service quotas"; 90-day TTL applies); the `channel` attribute plus a new `channel` filter on `GET /audit-logs` keeps the UI usable |
| D8 | Tool list filtering | `tools/list` is filtered by delivered phase and the mutating-tools switch only — **not** by principal; deterministic `(domain, name)` order; `CacheHint(ttl_ms=300_000, scope="private")` | Requirement 3.8 makes principal filtering optional; a principal-independent list is cacheable (SEP-2549), testable, and never wrong when a user's role changes mid-session. Authorization is always enforced at invocation |
| D9 | Portal mutating/destructive guardrails | `MCP_MUTATING_TOOLS_ENABLED` from CDK context `mcpMutatingToolsEnabled` (default **false**, `context-helpers.ts` pattern); destructive tools require `confirm` under **one rule on both servers (review-3 N2):** every `M!` `ToolSpec` sets exactly one of `confirm_target` (an argument name — `confirm` must equal `args[confirm_target]`; or, when the route takes its target through the branches of a top-level `oneOf`, a tuple naming one target per branch, and `confirm` must equal the target of the branch present — `portal_devices_deployment_create` → `('target_thing_group', 'target_devices[0]')`, revision 10, review-9 N7) or `confirm_literal` (a fixed string — `confirm` must equal it, used only when the tool acts on a device singleton with no target identifier: `edge_system_restart_dda` → `"restart-dda"`, `edge_system_snapshot_create` → `"snapshot"`, `edge_static_image_unpin` → `"static-image"`, `edge_static_video_unpin` → `"static-video"`); Use_Case tools require `usecase_id` explicitly | Requirements 5.3, 5.4, 5.6, 10.6, 14.2; annotations are untrusted so the gate must be server-side; one mechanical rule means the catalog, the validator and Property 2 cannot drift |
| D10 | Throttling (Decision point 5) | HTTP API `$default` stage throttle 20 rps / burst 40 (`HttpStage` `throttle`) plus per-route 10 rps / burst 20 on the six `/mcp*` route keys through a `CfnStage` `RouteSettings` property override; no per-principal limiter in this spec | Same mechanism as the Quick Setup routes (6.1); API Gateway usage plans need API keys (forbidden); because the gateway has no authorizer, the throttle is also what bounds unauthenticated Lambda invocations; `rate_limiter.evaluate` remains the reusable pure core if a per-`sub` limiter is ever needed (backlog) |
| D11 | Edge process model (Decision point 3) | **Child process from an isolated virtualenv baked into the image** (`/opt/dda/mcp-venv`, `mcp==2.3.0` + pinned transitive lock), supervised by the backend, **loopback-only** (`127.0.0.1:5790`), reached through a new `/mcp` route on the existing Backend_API listener that reverse-proxies to it | The only option that leaves `src/backend/requirements.txt` and `src/docker-compose.yaml` byte-identical (C7), avoids a third image/compose service/recipe lifecycle change, and isolates the SDK's floors from the Triton/vLLM/edgemlsdk stack. The venv's own uvicorn satisfies the SDK dependency without touching the backend's uvicorn 0.23.2 |
| D12 | Edge credential handling | The child validates nothing itself: it forwards the caller's credential to the Backend_API over loopback for every underlying route call (the existing `authorize_request`/`authorize_credential` decides) and obtains the principal from a new authenticated route `GET /local-auth/whoami`. For the five `download_file.py` image routes the credential is forwarded as the `token` query parameter those routes require (`credential_in_query`, H5) | Single validator (Requirement 9.1, 9.3, 9.8); the child never reads the credential cache, session secret, or authorization settings (10.8) and every call it makes is decided by the matrix |
| D13 | Edge role gate (F4, Decision point 5) | MCP-scoped policy in the child: Read_Only_Tools for every authenticated principal (and for the anonymous open-access row over loopback); Mutating_Tools only for `source == local_session` **and** role ∈ {Operator, UseCaseAdmin, PortalAdmin} **and** `McpMutatingToolsEnabled`; destructive tools additionally need `confirm`. Role-less principals (Existing_Token_Auth, anonymous) get read-only tools only | Requirement 10.2–10.6; the conservative role-less policy (10.5) closes F1's residual risk for state changes and is no more permissive than the Backend_API. Existing routes and the HMI are untouched (C7) |
| D14 | Edge exposure (12.4, F1) | `/mcp` is served to non-loopback peers only when the listener is the TLS one (`is_authorization_enabled_on_station()`); on the plaintext 5000 listener, non-loopback peers get 403 `mcp_loopback_only`. **Any `Origin` header → 403 `invalid_origin`** at the proxy (no browser client is intended; the proxy never forwards `Origin` to the child) **and at the child**, which is started with explicit `TransportSecuritySettings(enable_dns_rebinding_protection=True, allowed_hosts=['127.0.0.1:5790', 'localhost:5790'], allowed_origins=[])` instead of the SDK's loopback defaults (which would accept `http://127.0.0.1:*`/`http://localhost:*` Origins — review-3 M3). The rule rests on the assumption that Kiro sends no `Origin` header — validate-first item (viii); the fallback is an allow-list that is **empty by default** (`McpAllowedOrigins` component-configuration key on the edge, `MCP_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` on the portal — review-5 N5) | Satisfies 12.4 literally without a second listener or changing the Backend_API's TLS selection (12.7); the tunnel is the documented remote path (`docs/connect-to-device.md`); one Origin rule everywhere, now including direct loopback callers of the child (M6, review-3 M3) |
| D15 | Edge enablement (12.1, 12.2, 10.7) | Component-configuration keys `McpServerEnabled` (default `"false"`) and `McpMutatingToolsEnabled` (default `"false"`) read by `mcp_edge/config.py:McpConfig` through a new shared function `local_auth.config.read_component_config_via_ipc() -> dict` (one `DefectDetectionConfig` instance, one IPC connection, serialized by a lock) that `_read_local_login_enabled_via_ipc` also uses; `McpConfig` has its own 30 s poller; the supervisor starts/stops/restarts the child on transitions | Same mechanism as `LocalLoginEnabled` (D7 of portal-user-manager), 60 s bound honoured; no recipe reinstall; no second IPC connection (the recorded awscrt abort came from leaked connections, M4) |
| D16 | Edge logging (Requirement 11) | The child writes one JSON line per tool completion to stdout; the supervisor thread relays each as a structured `mcp.access` log record (same `structlog` pipeline as `api.access`); the proxy's correlation id is forwarded as `X-Request-ID` to the child and from the child to every underlying route call | One request id ties proxy entry, tool entry, and route entry for routes on `get_api_router()` (11.3); `download_file.py` routes produce no `api.access` record, so for those five tools correlation rests on the child's `mcp.access` record alone (documented, H5); logging failures are swallowed by the relay (11.4) |
| D17 | Protocol revisions | 2026-07-28 natively; handshake-era clients (2025-11-25, 2025-06-18, 2025-03-26) via the SDK's per-request routing with `stateless=True` and `json_response=True` on both servers; no HTTP+SSE (2024-11-05). **`MCP-Protocol-Version` header (Requirement 7.3; review-3 N6):** a 2026-07-28 request carries the header and the SDK validates it (an unknown value is a structured protocol-version rejection); a request **without** the header is handled as revision **2025-03-26** (`DEFAULT_NEGOTIATED_VERSION`, `mcp_types/_types.py:26`) — this is the specification's own backward-compatibility rule for pre-header clients, not a relaxation of 7.3: the server "requires" the header in the sense that any 2025-06-18+ or 2026-07-28 interaction is accepted only with a valid value, and the header-less path is the declared legacy support of Requirement 7.2 | Requirement 7.1–7.3; Lambda cannot hold legacy sessions or stream, so legacy requests are made self-contained; `json_response` suits buffered API Gateway responses; the absent-header rule is the SDK's, kept rather than patched so the compatibility tests (16.9) exercise real behaviour |
| D18 | SDK server class | Low-level `mcp.server.Server` with `on_list_tools`/`on_call_tool` driven by a declarative catalog (JSON Schema dicts), `cache_hints`, `version`, `instructions`; `MCPServer` decorators are not used | Catalog-driven servers need explicit schemas and deterministic lists; the low-level API is designed for exactly that and exposes the building blocks (`StreamableHTTPSessionManager`, auth middleware, PRM routes) the root-app composition needs |
| D19 | Portal dependencies | New Lambda layer `edge-cv-portal/backend/layers/mcp/` (`mcp==2.3.0`, `mangum==0.22.0`, built at CDK synth from a full lock with `pip install --platform manylinux2014_x86_64 --only-binary=:all: --python-version 3.11 --no-deps -t python/`); the existing `jwt` and `functions` requirement files stay byte-identical | Both are Security_Preservation_Gate-pinned; `mcp` already brings `pyjwt[crypto]` |
| D20 | Edge dependencies | `src/backend/mcp_edge/requirements.lock.txt`: **one lock resolved under Python 3.10**, pinning the same versions on every interpreter — notably `mcp==2.3.0`, `mcp-types==2.3.0`, **`rpds-py==0.30.0`** (last release with cp310 wheels) and **`starlette==1.7.0`** (last release supporting 3.10; `sse-starlette` 3.5.0 needs ≥0.49.1) — plus `exceptiongroup==1.3.1; python_version < "3.11"` as the only marker line; installed with `--only-binary :all:` into the venv in all five backend Dockerfiles | Requirement 13.2/13.5 (exact pins, wheels for cp310/cp311 aarch64 and x86_64, offline install). Two packages, not one, have dropped 3.10 (`rpds-py` at 2026.9.1, `starlette` at 1.8.0 — M7); pinning the 3.10-compatible versions everywhere gives identical behaviour on JP5/JP6/JP7/amd64 |
| D21 | Error vocabulary | Tool errors are `CallToolResult(is_error=True)` with text `"<category>: <message>"` and `structured_content {category, message, request_id, status?}` (`status` is the backend's HTTP status where there is one, except that both not-found outcomes of a `bind_usecase_path` spec carry `404` — D36; revision 12, review-11 N2); categories `not_authenticated, not_authorized, authorization_unavailable, not_found, invalid_input, confirmation_required, conflict, throttled, timeout, backend_failure, mutating_tools_disabled, not_available_on_device, result_too_large` | Requirements 1.5, 8.6, 14.1; models recover from `isError` results, not from protocol errors |
| D22 | Naming | `portal_<domain>_<object>_<verb>` and `edge_<object>_<verb>`, lowercase `[a-z0-9_]`, ≤ 64 chars; shared argument names `usecase_id, device_id, deployment_id, model_id, workflow_id, registration_id, execution_id, job_id, limit, offset, next_token, confirm` | Within the spec's allowed character set; one convention across both servers (14.1, 14.3) |
| D23 | Result bounds | `MAX_RESULT_BYTES = 200_000` per tool result, applied to the JSON **after** image extraction and field dropping (D34); collections are cut to fit with `truncated: true, returned, total_hint`; list tools take `limit` (default 50, max 200 — **or the narrower `ToolSpec.limit_default`/`limit_max` when the backing route's own bound is smaller**, e.g. `edge_workflow_images_list` max **2**, `edge_registration_executions_list` max 50, `edge_results_list` max 100, `portal_data_datasets_preview_get` max 50, `portal_data_captures_list` max 30 — review-6 M1/M5, revision 9) and `offset`/`next_token` — or `page` (≥ 1) where the route paginates by page number (`edge_results_list` → `page`/`size`) — forwarded through `param_map` when the route paginates (whenever it reads `limit`, `offset` or a cursor, `limit` and `offset` are forwarded, never sliced — revision 10, review-9 M2) and sliced by the dispatcher otherwise, in which case the spec offers both `limit` and `offset` (review-9 N4); a spec never offers a pagination argument its route cannot honour; images ≤ 2 MiB (`MAX_IMAGE_BYTES`) as `ImageContent` — from `image/*` bodies and from the base64 string a spec's `image_field` names inside a JSON body (review-5 M2) — larger → `result_too_large {bytes, limit}` | Requirements 1.6, 8.5; a default `limit` the route rejects (review-6 M1: `limit=50 → maxResults=50 → 400`) would make a Phase-1 list tool fail on every call |
| D24 | Edge vLLM capability (8.4) | The backend passes `--features vllm` to the child only when `app.VLLM_AVAILABLE` is true; text-generation tools exist in the catalog only when the feature flag is present | Mirrors the conditional router registration in `app.py:196` |
| D25 | **Request audit context (new; H6)** | `shared_utils.get_user_from_event(event)` records the event in a module-level `_REQUEST_AUDIT_CONTEXT` together with the current `_X_AMZN_TRACE_ID` value — except that, within one invocation, a remembered event carrying an `mcp` block is never replaced by one without it (a handler that synthesizes an in-process event, `synthetic_data.retrain_session` → `training.create_training_job`, 1252–1263, would otherwise unmark the entries written after it; revision 9); `_attribution_attributes(identity=None, event=None)` consults it and, **only when the remembered event belongs to the current invocation and carries an `mcp` block**, derives the five Attribution_Fields plus the marker from it (`identity_source` taken from `event['mcp']`, no registry read). Ordinary (browser) invocations record nothing new and behave exactly as today | Lambda processes one invocation at a time, so every audit write between a handler's `get_user_from_event(event)` and its return belongs to that invocation; the trace-id binding makes a stale context from a previous invocation inert even if a later invocation never calls `get_user_from_event`. Marks ~150 existing call sites without editing any handler (the rejected alternative — threading `event=event` through every call — touches ~40 handler files and would add a registry read to every browser-path audit write) |
| D26 | **Per-invocation ASGI composition (new; H2, H3)** | `mcp_portal/handler.py:handler(event, context)` calls `build_asgi_app()` on every invocation: six fresh `StreamableHTTPSessionManager(app=<module-level Server>, json_response=True, stateless=True, security_settings=…)`, six exact `POST`-only `Route`s wrapped in `RequireAuthMiddleware`, the eight root-level discovery routes from `mcp_portal/discovery.py` (root PRM, six path-suffixed PRMs, RFC 8414 facade — D27), root middleware **in this order — `AuthFailureAuditMiddleware` (outermost), `AuthenticationMiddleware(BearerAuthBackend(verifier))`, `AuthContextMiddleware`** (Starlette wraps the list in reverse, so the first entry is outermost and must enclose the verifier it audits — review-4 M1), and a lifespan that enters every manager's `run()` through an `AsyncExitStack`; then `Mangum(app, lifespan='auto')(event, context)`. `Server` objects, the catalog, the verifier (with its JWKS cache), and boto3 clients stay module-level | `StreamableHTTPSessionManager.run()` may be entered once per instance and Mangum runs the lifespan per invocation, so managers must be per invocation (H3); `Server.streamable_http_app()` puts the PRM route inside a per-view app, which `Mount` would move off the RFC 9728 path (H2). Composition costs microseconds and no I/O |
| D27 | **Portal discovery path (new; review-2 H1, M1)** | **Well-known URIs are the discovery mechanism; the RFC 8414 facade is the authorization-server metadata.** The MCP Lambda serves, unauthenticated: `GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource` (root; the canonical `/mcp` document), `GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/mcp[/<view>]` (six path-suffixed documents), and `GET /.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` (a static RFC 8414 document with `issuer = MCP_AS_ISSUER` = the HTTP API origin `https://<id>.execute-api.<region>.amazonaws.com`, Cognito hosted-UI `authorization_endpoint`/`token_endpoint`/`revocation_endpoint`, Cognito `jwks_uri`, `code_challenge_methods_supported: ["S256"]`). Every PRM's `authorization_servers` is `[MCP_AS_ISSUER]`. All three document kinds are rendered by the new `mcp_portal/discovery.py` from environment strings (not by the SDK's `create_protected_resource_routes`). The SDK's 401 `WWW-Authenticate` challenge is still emitted but **not relied upon**; a deployed-stack smoke check records what the client actually receives. Token validation is unchanged (`iss` must equal the **Cognito** issuer) | The MCP specification requires one of header-based or well-known-URI discovery and obliges clients to fall back to the well-known URIs (path-suffixed, then root); API Gateway remaps `WWW-Authenticate`, so the header path is unavailable without a CloudFront distribution, and Kiro's own troubleshooting guide fetches the root PRM. Kiro documents RFC 8414 as the required authorization-server metadata location and both Cognito RFC 8414 locations answer 400; Cognito's OIDC document lacks `code_challenge_methods_supported`, which MCP clients MUST treat as "no PKCE" and refuse — the facade removes both blockers with ~30 lines and no new trust (its endpoints are the existing hosted UI; tokens are still Cognito-issued and verified against Cognito's JWKS and issuer). `discovery.py` owns the JSON because a pre-built `AnyHttpUrl("https://host")` gains a trailing slash (`https://host/`) before `ProtectedResourceMetadata`'s `url_preserve_empty_path=True` can preserve it (verified with pydantic 2.14.1, 2026-10-18), and Kiro's exact issuer match would then fail; emitting the same `MCP_AS_ISSUER` string into both documents makes Requirement 7.5 a one-line unit test |
| D28 | **Edge child trust boundary (new; review-3 M3)** | The child is **bound to loopback, which under `network_mode: host` is the device's shared loopback — reachable by any local process, not only the Backend_API**. The design therefore does not treat the proxy as a security boundary: the child **never acts on its own authority** — every `whoami` and every underlying route call carries the caller's own credential to the Backend_API, where `authorize_request`/`authorize_credential` decide — so a direct loopback caller gains nothing it could not get from the Backend_API itself. What a direct call *does* skip is the proxy-level `api.access` record for the `/mcp` request, the proxy's exposure rule (moot on loopback) and the proxy's 1 MiB body cap; the child closes the last gap itself (`max_request_body_size=MCP_PROXY_MAX_BODY`) and applies the same Origin rule (D14), and every tool completion is still recorded by the child's own `mcp.access` line and, for routes on `get_api_router()`, by the Backend_API's `api.access` line. A Unix-domain-socket bind (which *would* confine the child to the container) is recorded as hardening (Backlog 11), not adopted for the MVP | Correct statement of the boundary replaces the false "reachable only from the Backend_API process"; the authentication invariant holds because the credential, not the network path, is what the Backend_API checks (Requirements 9.1, 10.8, 12.5; Property 16); explicit `TransportSecuritySettings` costs one line and removes the SDK's permissive loopback `allowed_origins` default |
| D29 | **`PORTAL_REGISTRY_ENFORCED=false` and `global`-scoped tools (new; review-3 M2)** | **Recommendation: ship Phase 1 with the limitation documented and an explicit owner decision (Phase 1 sign-off item (e)).** With enforcement off, every `G`-scoped tool resolves a non-PortalAdmin MCP principal to **Viewer** (Key research findings), so the tools whose permission a non-PortalAdmin role holds at the global scope — the labeler self-service tools (`labeling:tasks-self`), the build tools (`builds:read\|submit\|cancel`), and any `usecase_or_global` tool invoked without `usecase_id` (none since revision 9) — are usable through MCP only by PortalAdmins until `PORTAL_REGISTRY_ENFORCED=true` **and** the principal has a `global` registry row naming the role (the same row the web UI needs under enforcement). Requirement 3.6 (DataLabeler reaches the labeler tools) is therefore met under enforcement **on** and **not** under enforcement off; MCP is never more permissive than the web UI in either mode. Alternatives offered to the owner at sign-off: (B) require `portalRegistryEnforced=true` wherever `deployMcpServer` is on (a synth-time check in `bin/app.ts`); (C) have the MCP Lambda read the principal's Cognito `custom:role` attribute through the `AdminGetUser` call it already makes for the email and place it in the forwarded claims and in `user_info['role']` — this reproduces the web UI's legacy resolution exactly in both modes but adds a claim the access token did not carry, which reopens the Requirement 3.9 "claims verbatim" interpretation (sign-off item (a)) and Property 5 (Backlog 12) | Enforcement on is already the portal's target state and the design's recommendation; (A) keeps C4 ("never more") trivially true and needs no new code path; (B) couples MCP adoption to the registry backfill; (C) is the only option that fully satisfies 3.6 under enforcement off but changes a sign-off item the owner has not yet confirmed — the owner, not the design, should make that trade |
| D30 | **Backend dispatch map carrier (new; review-4 H1)** | The backend-key → function-ARN map (43 entries: 32 `ComputeStack` handlers incl. the nested `ApiGatewayStack`'s component-configuration handler, 7 `NodeDesignerStack`, 3 `BuildFleetStack`, 1 `SyntheticDataStack`) is **not** a Lambda environment variable. `mcp-stack.ts` writes it to an **Advanced-tier SSM `StringParameter`** `/dda-portal/mcp/backend-functions` (`stringValue: cdk.Stack.of(this).toJsonString(...)`); the Lambda environment carries only `MCP_BACKEND_FUNCTIONS_PARAMETER` (the parameter name); `dispatch.py` loads the map **once per container, lazily on the first tool call**, with `ssm:GetParameter` (a sixth exact-ARN statement on `McpPortalRole`) | 40 of the 43 backing functions have CloudFormation-generated names, so their ARNs are 107–111 characters; the three fixed-name handlers (`CameraVideoPinHandler` `dda-portal-camera-video-pin`, `compute-stack.ts:2624`; `WorkflowGeneratorHandler` `dda-portal-workflow-generator`, `:3162`; `SyntheticDataHandler` `dda-synthetic-data-handler`, `synthetic-data-stack.ts:397`) are shorter (≈ 75 characters) — the JSON map is ≈ 5.5 KB either way (review-5 N1) — over Lambda's **4 KB aggregate** environment-variable quota on its own (even the 32 `ComputeStack` ARNs alone are ≈ 4.0 KB), before the ≈ 1.2 KB of other MCP variables; `CreateFunction`/`UpdateFunctionConfiguration` would reject the configuration and the Phase-1 deploy would roll back. A standard-tier parameter (4 KB) is too small; the advanced tier (8 KB) leaves ~2 KB of headroom for catalog growth. The rejected alternative — a compact key → `functionName` map (~2.7 KB) with ARNs rebuilt at runtime from `context.invoked_function_arn` — would keep the environment within ~0.5 KB of the quota and couple it to the catalog's size; SSM removes the coupling at the cost of one small `GetParameter` call per cold start |
| D31 | **MCP stack owns its shared layer (new; review-4 H2)** | `mcp-stack.ts` creates `new lambda.LayerVersion(this, 'McpSharedLayer', { code: lambda.Code.fromAsset(path.join(__dirname, '../../backend/layers/shared')), compatibleRuntimes: [PYTHON_3_11] })` from the **same asset** `ComputeStack`'s `SharedLayer` uses; the `sharedLayer` prop and the proposed `ComputeStack.sharedLayer` public member are **dropped**. Only ARNs that are stable across updates (functions, tables, user pool) are imported across stacks | `AWS::Lambda::LayerVersion` is replaced with a new ARN on every content change; importing `ComputeStack`'s layer ARN would make CDK export it from `EdgeCVPortalComputeStack`, and CloudFormation refuses to modify an exported value another stack imports — so the first `deploy-infrastructure.sh` run after Phase 1 that touches `layers/shared/python/*` (D25 is itself such a change; the file changes often) would fail `ComputeStack`'s update until the MCP stack was destroyed. `NodeDesignerStack`, `BuildFleetStack` and `SyntheticDataStack` already follow the own-copy pattern for exactly this reason (Key research findings); `cdk deploy --all` builds both copies from the same working tree, so a shared-layer change lands in every stack in one run |
| D32 | **Single-attempt Lambda invoke, `status_tool`, and a budget derived from the remaining invocation time (new; review-4 M2, review-5 M3)** | `dispatch.py` builds its client once: `LAMBDA = boto3.client('lambda', config=botocore.config.Config(connect_timeout=2, read_timeout=20, retries={'total_max_attempts': 1}))` — exactly one `Invoke` attempt per tool call, never an SDK retry. **Time budget:** the MCP Lambda's timeout is **29 s** (the HTTP API integration limit is 30 s); `handler(event, context)` stores the Lambda `context` in `dispatch.LAMBDA_CONTEXT` before Mangum runs, and immediately before `Invoke` the dispatcher checks `LAMBDA_CONTEXT.get_remaining_time_in_millis() < MIN_DISPATCH_BUDGET_MS` (**24 000**) → `ToolFailure('timeout', 'insufficient time budget to start the backing operation; retry')` with the **plain** message (nothing ran, so the effect is known) and no `Invoke`; when no context is set (in-memory test client) the guard is skipped. Arithmetic: 29 − 24 = 5 s is the allowance for cold-start imports, the first-call JWKS fetch, `ssm:GetParameter`, `AdminGetUser` and the two registry reads; 24 − (2 + 20) = 2 s remains after the worst-case invoke for the envelope `PutItem`, the telemetry line and Mangum's response. Every `M`/`M!` `ToolSpec` (both servers) carries `status_tool`: the `RO` tool that observes the affected resource. For a Mutating_Tool, the post-dispatch `timeout` error and any post-dispatch `backend_failure` (transport error, `FunctionError`, malformed response) read `"<category>: the operation may have been applied; call <status_tool> before retrying"`. The edge child's `httpx2` client and the proxy's `httpx` 0.24.1 client are constructed with the default `retries=0` and the design asserts it | botocore retries `Invoke` after a client-side `ReadTimeoutError` in both retry modes (Research baseline); with the read timeout below several backing handlers' own timeouts (`DeploymentsHandler` 60 s, `compute-stack.ts:1246–1262`; `ComponentsHandler` 60 s, `:1535–1543`; `TrainingHandler` 120 s, `:1465`; `ComponentConfigurationHandler` 60 s, `api-gateway-stack.ts:711–719`), a slow `POST /deployments`, `/components/configure` or `/training` would time out client-side while the backing Lambda kept running and the SDK would invoke it **again** — two deployments from one tool call (Requirement 5 destructive safeguards). One attempt plus an explicit "effect unknown — observe first" message is the only configuration under which a mutating tool has at most one effect and the model is steered to check before retrying. The budget guard exists because revision 5's 2 s + 25 s invoke could outlive the 28 s function on a cold container: Lambda would kill the MCP function before botocore's `ReadTimeoutError` fired, Mangum would never answer, the client would see API Gateway's bare 5xx (no `isError`, no `effect: unknown` steering, no `mcp_tool_call` envelope, no `mcp_tool` line — Requirements 4.1, 6.3), and its natural retry would produce the second effect this decision exists to prevent (review-5 M3). With the guard, a backing call is started only when it can finish — or time out client-side — inside the function's own lifetime |
| D33 | **Opt-out is destroy-then-deploy (new; review-4 M3)** | Disabling the MCP server on a deployed portal is **two steps**: (1) from `infrastructure/`, `npx cdk destroy EdgeCVPortalMcpStack --force` with the stack still in the app (no `deployMcpServer=false` context; `--force` skips `cdk destroy`'s interactive "Are you sure?" prompt — without it the operator answers `y` — review-5 N4), which also deletes the MCP app client, resource server and SSM parameter; (2) `DEPLOY_MCP_SERVER=false ./deploy-infrastructure.sh`. **One rule for the variable (review-5 N4):** `portal_mcp_optout_guard` applies the `mcpServerEnabled` rule in shell — `DEPLOY_MCP_SERVER` is **off** iff its value, trimmed and lower-cased, is exactly `false` (the `groundedSamWorkerEnabled` default-ON semantics the CDK helper uses) — and only an **off** value makes the script append `-c deployMcpServer=false` to `CDK_CONTEXT_ARGS`; any other value (`true`, unset, empty, `0`, `no`) appends nothing, so the CDK default (on) applies. `deploy-infrastructure.sh` gains the guard (`scripts/portal-mcp-stack.sh:portal_mcp_optout_guard`): when the value is off **and** `aws cloudformation describe-stacks --stack-name EdgeCVPortalMcpStack` succeeds, it aborts with the step-(1) instruction | `cdk deploy --all` never destroys a stack that merely disappeared from the app; with the flag false, `EdgeCVPortalComputeStack`'s template drops the 32 handler-ARN exports the still-deployed MCP stack imports (and the other three producing stacks drop theirs), so `ComputeStack`'s own update fails ("Export … cannot be deleted as it is in use by EdgeCVPortalMcpStack") and the whole portal deploy stops. The former one-flag reversibility claim (C7) was wrong as stated; `computeStack.exportValue(...)` retention is recorded only as a fallback for an operator who has already hit the error |
| D34 | **Edge result hygiene: path scrub, image fields, dropped fields, oversized-image guard (new; review-5 H1, M1, M2; review-6 M2, N2)** | Four rules, driven by edge `ToolSpec` fields, applied by `child/shaping.py` to every 2xx JSON body **before** the size bound. **Field paths** (review-6 M2) are **root-relative dotted paths** in which `[]` means "every element of the array at this position" — `logoImage`, `executions`, `inferenceResult.mask_image`, `images[].image`, `results[].maskImage`, and `[].artifactPath` for a body that is itself an array (`GET /workflows/registrations`, `api.py:160`) — the same notation `scrubbed_fields`/`dropped_fields` already use to report what changed; a path that resolves to nothing is a no-op. **(a) `scrub_paths: bool = True`** — a recursive walk replaces the value of every key matching `PATH_KEY_RE = ^(.*[Pp]ath\|.*[Dd]ir(ectory)?\|.*[Ll]ocation\|.*[Ff]ile[Nn]ame\|.*[Ff]older)$` whose value is *path-like* (a string containing `/` that is not a non-`file:` URL, i.e. does not match `^(?!file:)[a-z][a-z0-9+.-]*://`), **and** every string value anywhere that is a **single device-root path token** — matches `^(file://)?/(aws_dda\|tmp\|opt\|home\|root\|var\|etc\|usr\|snapshot)/\S*$` (no whitespace, so a log line or message that merely *begins* with a device path is left alone — review-6 N2) — with **its file name only** (`os.path.basename` after stripping `file://` and a trailing `/`; an empty basename drops the key), and records each affected key path in `structured_content.scrubbed_fields`; the `{"text": …}` wrapper that §7 step 5 builds for non-JSON bodies (`edge_execution_log_get`) is **exempt from the walk** — it is free text, not a field; a spec may set `scrub_paths=False` only with a row note (none does); **(b) `image_field: str \| None = None`** — when it names a non-empty string in the body, the string is base64-decoded (an optional `data:image/…;base64,` prefix stripped), the decoded bytes are sniffed (`FF D8 FF` → `image/jpeg`, `\x89PNG` → `image/png`, `BM` → `image/bmp`, `RIFF…WEBP` → `image/webp`, else `image/jpeg`), and: decoded size ≤ `MAX_IMAGE_BYTES` (2 MiB) → `ImageContent(data=<the original base64>, mime_type=<sniffed>)` is appended to `content` and the field is deleted from `result`; larger → `result_too_large {bytes, limit}`; undecodable → `backend_failure` ("image field is not valid base64"); `null`/absent → nothing; a spec with `image_field` may also set **`result_constant: dict \| None`**, a constant object merged into `result` **immediately after extraction — before (d) and the scrub, so a constant is subject to the same guard and scrub as any other field** (review-7 N1: one order, stated once here and repeated in §5 and §7) — so a body the image consumed entirely does not shape to `{}` (`edge_image_source_capture` → `{"captured": true}`); **(c) `drop_fields: tuple[str, ...] = ()`** — the named paths are removed before the size bound and recorded in `structured_content.dropped_fields`; **(d) the generic oversized-image guard (review-6 M2)** — after (a)–(c), any remaining string value of ≥ 16 KiB whose first 16 base64 characters (a `data:image/…;base64,` prefix stripped) decode to bytes starting with one of the image magic numbers of (b) is replaced by `"<dropped: <n> bytes image>"` (`n` = the string's length) and its path recorded in `dropped_fields` — so the next base64 carrier the survey overlooks degrades a result instead of failing it. The `image/*`-body rule of §7 step 5 is unchanged (`image/jpg` normalized to `image/jpeg`). **(e) Text tail (review-7 N6):** a spec whose route returns a `text/plain` body may set **`text_tail_arg: str \| None`** (`'tail_chars'` on `edge_execution_log_get` only): the child keeps the **longest suffix of at most** `args[text_tail_arg]` characters (schema `integer`, 1–150 000, default 150 000) **whose encoding `json.dumps({"text": suffix})` is ≤ `MAX_RESULT_BYTES − 1 000` bytes**, wraps it as `{"text": …}`, and records `truncated: true` and `total_chars: <original length>` in `structured_content` when anything was cut — a character count alone cannot bound the encoded size (review-8 N4): `read_run_log` decodes with `errors="replace"` (`run_artifacts.py:397`) and Run_Log lines are free text, so a newline or quote encodes to 2 bytes and U+FFFD, a control or non-ASCII character to 6 (150 000 `"`+newline characters encode to 300 012 B, 90 000 U+FFFD to 540 012 B); the suffix is found by binary search over the start offset — so a Run_Log of up to ≈ 1 MiB (two 512 KiB rotation files, Key research findings) is reachable by its newest part instead of failing as `result_too_large`; the argument is consumed by the child and never forwarded (the server-consumed-argument rule, Property 2). `MAX_RESULT_BYTES` then applies to what remains | `GET /snapshot`, `list_images`, the registration/execution dicts and the results export all return device paths (Key research findings), so "never returns a filesystem path" (Requirement 8.5, §7 step 5) was a claim without a mechanism (review-5 M1); a file name shows nothing of the directory layout and keeps the results usable — `<capture_id>.jsonl` is how `edge_workflow_images_list` items are correlated with the id-addressed capture image tools, and `snapshot-<station>-<ts>.tar.gz` is what an operator needs to find the archive. `preview_image`, `test_stream_connection` and `get_workflow_execution_overlay` carry their frames as base64 *inside* JSON, so the `image/*` rule never fired and any frame above ~150 KB would have become `result_too_large` (review-5 M2); extracting the named field makes the catalog's "→ `ImageContent`" true and keeps the 2 MiB bound. The sixth review found five more carriers (`logoImage`, `inferenceResult.mask_image`, `results[].maskImage`, `maskImage`, the run route's `image`) and one unbounded embedded list (`executions`) that the top-level-only `drop_fields` of revision 6 could not reach — hence dotted paths, and the guard in (d) because a survey of response models is exactly the kind of list that goes stale. One generic walk is simpler to test (Properties 9 and 19) than per-tool shapers |
| D35 | **Rendered tool descriptions and listed aliases (new; review-5 M4)** | `to_mcp_tool(spec)` (both servers) renders `description = f"{spec.description} Touches: {spec.resource_type}{scope_text}. {classification_text}"` where `spec.description` is a non-empty sentence ending in `.` (≤ 300 characters), `scope_text` is `" in the Use_Case named by usecase_id"` when `spec.usecase_arg` is set (portal) and `" on this device"` on the edge, and `classification_text` is **exactly one of** `"Read-only."`, `"MUTATING."`, or `f"MUTATING, DESTRUCTIVE — requires confirm={spec.confirm_target or repr(spec.confirm_literal)}."` (e.g. `requires confirm=workflow_id`, `requires confirm='restart-dda'`). Every spec with `deprecated_alias` also sets `alias_removed_in: str` (semver); `tools_for_view`/the edge list emit an **additional** `Tool(name=spec.deprecated_alias, title=spec.title, description=f"Deprecated alias of {spec.name}; removed in {spec.alias_removed_in}. {rendered description}", input_schema=spec.input_schema, annotations=<same>)`, included iff its target is included and sorted by its own name within `(domain, name)`; calling the alias runs the target and the result carries `deprecation` text (§4 step 2). `ToolSpec.resource_type: str` (e.g. `device`, `model`, `workflow`, `collection`) is the field `audit.py` already records | Requirement 5.1 puts the classification "in the tool description text", 1.2 wants "which Portal resources it touches", and 14.4 wants a removed name kept "with a deprecation note in its description" — none of which the catalog tables or `to_mcp_tool` specified, so an implementer would have invented three rules (review-5 M4). Rendering from the fields the catalog already has (`resource_type`, `classification`, `destructive`, `confirm_*`) means the text cannot drift from the annotations, and Property 2 can check it mechanically; listing the alias is the only way a client that reads `tools/list` can learn it exists |
| D36 | **Use_Case binding of id-addressed reads (new; revision 10, review-9 N5)** | `ToolSpec.bind_usecase_path: str \| None` — a dotted path into the 2xx body naming the object's own Use_Case. After a 2xx the shaper compares that value with the `usecase_id` the pre-dispatch check authorized; on a mismatch (or an absent value) it drops the body and returns `not_found` "no such object in the named Use_Case", and the envelope is written with `result='denied'`, `details.reason='usecase_binding'`; both outcomes — the refusal and a backend 404 on such a spec — return `ToolFailure('not_found', 'no such object in the named Use_Case', status=404)`, the 404 instead of the handler's own text (`labeling.py:678` "Labeling job not found"), so a missing id and another Use_Case's id give identical results: the whole `CallToolResult` (`content` and `structured_content`) is the same except `request_id`, and the real backend status (the 2xx or the 404) is kept only in the `mcp_tool` log line and the envelope's `details.status` (revision 11, review-10 N3; revision 12, review-11 N2). Set on `portal_data_labeling_job_get` (`'job.usecase_id'`); `portal_data_labeling_manifest_get`, whose body carries no Use_Case, is not in `CATALOG` until Backlog 18's handler-side binding lands; the id-addressed Greengrass reads, whose objects carry no portal Use_Case in the body, are sign-off item (j) | Requirements 3.3, 1.3; the pre-dispatch check authorizes the **named** Use_Case, so an object that a handler reads by id alone is bound to that Use_Case here; `not_found` (not `not_authorized`) does not confirm the id exists, and the `denied` envelope records the refusal |

## Architecture

### Portal

```mermaid
graph TB
    subgraph Developer machine
        K[Kiro IDE / CLI<br/>mcp.json: url + oauth]
    end
    subgraph Cognito user pool dda-portal-users - existing
        HUI[Hosted UI /oauth2/authorize + /oauth2/token<br/>PKCE, resource=endpoint URL]
        RS[(Resource server<br/>id = canonical MCP URL<br/>scope portal.tools - NEW, created in McpStack)]
        CL[(App client dda-portal-mcp-client<br/>public, code grant - NEW, created in McpStack)]
    end
    subgraph EdgeCVPortalMcpStack - NEW
        API[HTTP API dda-portal-mcp<br/>$default stage, throttled<br/>NO gateway authorizer]
        L[McpPortalHandler Lambda<br/>mcp 2.3.0 + mangum, app composed per invocation<br/>BearerAuthBackend + CognitoAccessTokenVerifier<br/>catalog, dispatcher, audit envelope<br/>layers: McpSharedLayer own copy + McpLayer]
        SSM[(SSM parameter /dda-portal/mcp/backend-functions<br/>Advanced tier, backend key to handler ARN map<br/>read once per container)]
    end
    subgraph EdgeCVPortalComputeStack / ApiGatewayStack / NodeDesignerStack / BuildFleetStack / SyntheticDataStack - existing
        H1[DevicesHandler]
        H2[ModelsHandler]
        H3[WorkflowsHandler]
        Hn[... other route handlers]
    end
    DDB[(dda-portal-user-roles<br/>Portal_Identity_Registry)]
    AUD[(Audit log table<br/>+ channel / mcp_* attributes)]

    K -->|401 then GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource[/mcp] -> GET /.well-known/oauth-authorization-server facade| API
    K -->|/oauth2/authorize + /oauth2/token from the facade document| HUI
    HUI --> RS
    HUI --> CL
    K -->|Authorization: Bearer access token| API
    API -->|every request| L
    L -->|ssm:GetParameter on first tool call| SSM
    L -->|rbac_manager.has_permission| DDB
    L -->|lambda:InvokeFunction, one attempt, claims verbatim + event.mcp| H1
    L --> H2
    L --> H3
    L --> Hn
    L -->|mcp_tool_call / unauthorized_access / authentication_failed| AUD
    H1 -->|own audit entries marked via request audit context| AUD
```

### Edge

```mermaid
graph TB
    subgraph Client
        K2[Kiro / MCP client<br/>headers: Authorization Bearer ${DDA_EDGE_TOKEN}]
    end
    subgraph flask-app container - network_mode host
        subgraph Backend_API process - app.py, uvicorn 0.23.2, :5000 or :5443 TLS
            AR[authorize_request<br/>decision matrix, unchanged]
            PX[POST /mcp - NEW<br/>Origin check, loopback/TLS rule, reverse proxy]
            WHO[GET /local-auth/whoami - NEW]
            ST[GET /mcp/status - NEW]
            SUP[McpSupervisor thread - NEW<br/>McpConfig poller, child lifecycle, log relay]
            ROUTES[existing routers<br/>/workflows /cameras /system-health ...]
            DL[download_file.py image routes<br/>token query parameter, unchanged]
        end
        subgraph Child process - /opt/dda/mcp-venv python, 127.0.0.1:5790
            MCP[dda-edge-mcp<br/>mcp 2.3.0 low-level Server<br/>catalog, role gate, JSON-line log]
        end
    end
    CFG[Greengrass component configuration<br/>McpServerEnabled, McpMutatingToolsEnabled]
    CACHE[(Local_Credential_Cache<br/>+ session secret - read only by Backend_API)]

    LOCAL[any local process<br/>shared loopback under network_mode host]
    K2 -->|LAN only when TLS listener;<br/>otherwise SSH / IoT tunnel to loopback| PX
    LOCAL -.->|direct POST 127.0.0.1:5790/mcp - no proxy api.access record<br/>Origin 403, body cap 413, credential still decided by Backend_API| MCP
    PX --> AR
    PX -->|forward allowlisted headers + X-Request-ID| MCP
    MCP -->|GET /local-auth/whoami, same bearer| WHO
    MCP -->|tool call = underlying route, same bearer, same X-Request-ID| ROUTES
    MCP -->|image tools: ?token= over loopback| DL
    WHO --> AR
    ROUTES --> AR
    DL -->|authorize_credential| CACHE
    AR --> CACHE
    CFG -->|IPC GetConfiguration via shared reader, 30 s| SUP
    SUP -->|spawn / stop / restart| MCP
    MCP -->|stdout JSON lines| SUP
    SUP -->|mcp.access structlog| ST
```

### Portal tool invocation sequence

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as MCP client
    participant G as HTTP API (no authorizer, throttled)
    participant M as McpPortalHandler
    participant R as Portal_Identity_Registry
    participant H as Route handler Lambda
    participant A as Audit log

    C->>G: POST /mcp tools/call (Bearer access token, MCP-Protocol-Version)
    G->>M: event v2 (headers, sourceIp, userAgent)
    M->>M: build_asgi_app(); BearerAuthBackend -> CognitoAccessTokenVerifier
    alt no / invalid token
        M-->>C: 401 (SDK adds WWW-Authenticate; the HTTP API passes it through unchanged, observed 2026-10-25; a REST API would remap it to X-Amzn-Remapped-WWW-Authenticate)
        C->>G: GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/mcp (fallback), then GET /.well-known/oauth-authorization-server
        M->>A: authentication_failed (only when the signature verified and sub is known)
    else scope missing
        M-->>C: 403 insufficient_scope
    else authenticated
        M->>M: catalog lookup, argument validation, confirm / mutating switch
        M->>R: has_permission(sub, usecase_id, permission)
        alt denied
            M->>A: unauthorized_access (channel=mcp, mcp_tool, ...)
            M-->>C: isError not_authorized
        else registry unavailable
            M->>A: authorization_unavailable (failure)
            M-->>C: isError authorization_unavailable
        else allowed
            M->>H: Invoke(event v1: method/resource/path, claims verbatim, event.mcp)
            H->>H: get_user_from_event(event) remembers the request audit context
            H->>H: own RBAC + validation + effect (+ own audit, marked by the context)
            H-->>M: {statusCode, body}
            M->>A: mcp_tool_call (only when handler_audits = false, or on failure)
            M-->>C: structured_content / isError by status
        end
    end
```

## Resolution of the eight open design decisions

### Decision point 1 — Portal MCP topology

**Recommendation: one deployable with domain views (D1).** The three options were (a) one monolithic server, (b) several independently deployed domain servers, (c) one deployable exposing several MCP server identities. (b) multiplies the expensive parts — Cognito client/resource configuration, audit plumbing, CDK stacks, Kiro logins (each server URL is its own OAuth resource) — for no isolation gain, because every domain is gated by the same RBAC_Layer anyway. (a) is the cheapest but puts 87 Phase-1 tools (184 in all, revision 10) into every client's context. (c) costs one `Route` + one session manager per view and gives the "MCP servers" the owner asked for: a Kiro user configures `…/mcp/devices` and sees only device/deployment tools, or `…/mcp` for everything. All views are served by the same Lambda, share the same catalog objects (identical names, schemas, annotations: Requirement 14), the same token verifier (the accepted `aud` set is the deployable's six endpoint URLs, so one login covers all views), and the same audit path. The views and their domains: `data` (datasets, data management, labeling, synthetic data), `models` (models, import/convert, training, compilation), `devices` (devices, device logs, camera registry, deployments, components, shared components, device registrations), `workflows` (workflows, validation, test runs, packaging, node designer, builds), `admin` (whoami, use cases, roles, audit logs, accounts).

### Decision point 2 — Portal hosting, transport, and authentication

**Recommendation: (a) remote Streamable HTTP on Lambda behind a dedicated HTTP API with no gateway authorizer, authenticated inside the Lambda with Cognito access tokens from a new MCP app client + resource server, discovered through well-known Protected Resource Metadata and an RFC 8414 facade served by the same Lambda; no stdio package now (D2, D3, D4, D26, D27).**

*Why remote and not stdio/both.* Kiro IDE and CLI both implement the OAuth client side (PKCE, loopback redirect, refresh) for remote servers; a stdio package would re-implement Cognito login on every laptop, need its own token storage, and — per the MCP spec — take credentials from the environment, which for Cognito means a user pasting a short-lived token. Remote deploys once with the portal, is versioned with it, and keeps every secret-bearing step inside Cognito's hosted UI. A stdio bridge for clients without OAuth support is a backlog item.

*Why a dedicated HTTP API and not the existing REST API.* (1) RFC 9728 puts Protected Resource Metadata at `https://host/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource<path>`; a REST API's stage prefix (`/v1/...`) makes that location unroutable; an HTTP API `$default` stage has no prefix. (2) The existing `ApiGatewayStack` nested stack sat at the 500-resource limit before the ARN-imported integrations removed the per-method permissions (345 resources at the latest synth, review-5 N2), so by the convention that history established, new route groups go into yet another nested route stack; `ComputeStack` itself has room (652,017 bytes / 248 resources at the latest synth, ~350 KB under the 1 MB template limit — review-4 N7), so template size is **not** a reason, and the 32 handler-ARN exports this design adds to it (~7 KB; 43 across all four producing stacks) are safe. (3) C7/C8: no change to the existing API's gateway responses, throttles, or authorizers.

*Discovery: well-known URIs, not the 401 header (review-2 H1; D27).* The MCP specification lets a server choose between the `WWW-Authenticate … resource_metadata` header and serving Protected Resource Metadata at well-known URIs, and obliges clients that see no header to construct and fetch the well-known URIs themselves (path-suffixed first, then root). API Gateway remaps the `WWW-Authenticate` response header of Lambda integrations to `X-Amzn-Remapped-WWW-Authenticate` (documented for REST APIs, reproduced in the field on HTTP API v2 and Lambda Function URLs — Research baseline), and HTTP API response mappings cannot read `x-amzn-*` headers, so the header mechanism is **not available** on this hosting without a CloudFront distribution. This design therefore implements mechanism (ii): the Lambda serves the root PRM (`GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource`, the canonical `/mcp` document — the form Kiro's troubleshooting guide fetches) **and** the six path-suffixed PRMs (`/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/mcp[/<view>]`, the form the spec's fallback tries first), all routed through the HTTP API to the same Lambda. The SDK's `RequireAuthMiddleware` still answers 401 with `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer error="invalid_token", error_description="Authentication required", resource_metadata="<that endpoint's PRM URL>"` (its `_send_auth_error` emits exactly those three parameters, never the optional `scope=` hint); if a future gateway configuration passes it through, clients simply skip the fallback. **Documented deviation from Requirement 2.4's header wording:** as deployed, the client receives the challenge under the remapped name, so the "answer unauthenticated requests with HTTP 401 and a `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer resource_metadata=…` challenge" clause is met at the Lambda boundary and not at the client boundary; the server conforms to the specification through the well-known-URI mechanism. This is an owner sign-off item (Phasing, Phase 1). Two remedies were considered and rejected for the MVP: an HTTP API integration `ResponseParameters` mapping for status 401 (`overwrite:header.WWW-Authenticate ← $response.header.x-amzn-remapped-www-authenticate`) is excluded by the reserved-header rule, and a variant that copies the challenge into a non-reserved header (`X-DDA-Authenticate`) and maps it back would make 2.4 compliance depend on undocumented remap ordering; a **CloudFront distribution** in front of the HTTP API with a response-headers function (the field report's workaround) restores the header reliably but adds a distribution, a certificate/domain decision and a second URL to document — it is the designed remedy **if the owner declines the deviation**, and is otherwise Backlog 10. A **deployed-stack smoke check** (`curl -si -X POST https://<id>.execute-api.<region>.amazonaws.com/mcp -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{}'`; record the status line and every `*www-authenticate*` header name in the Phase-1 evidence) is a Phase-1 entry criterion so the deviation is recorded as observed, not assumed. A side benefit of D27: the field report (anthropics/claude-ai-mcp#748, verified 2026-10-18) observed that a client which never sees the header may "fall back to treating our server's own origin as the authorization server" and request `<server-origin>/authorize…`; in this design the MCP server's origin **is** the advertised authorization server and serves RFC 8414 metadata at the standard location, so even that degraded client behaviour converges on the facade and the correct Cognito endpoints.

*Why no gateway authorizer (H4, re-justified).* All routes use `HttpNoneAuthorizer`; the SDK's `AuthenticationMiddleware(BearerAuthBackend(CognitoAccessTokenVerifier()))` + `RequireAuthMiddleware` inside the Lambda is the single validator: missing/invalid token → 401 (challenge as above), scope absent → 403 `insufficient_scope`. The verifier performs every check a gateway authorizer would (`kid` lookup in the pool's JWKS, RS256 signature, `iss`, `exp`, `aud`/`client_id`) plus `token_use == 'access'` and the scope rule, so nothing is lost; what is gained is Requirement 4.7's marked `authentication_failed` audit entry for claim-level failures (a gateway authorizer would discard them before any code of ours ran) and one in-process-testable validator. The cost — unauthenticated requests invoke the Lambda — is bounded by the stage/route throttles (D10) and by the verifier's short-circuit (no network call for a missing or unparseable token; JWKS cached per container). The CDK test enumerates the route set and asserts it is exactly the documented Lambda-authenticated exception set (Requirement 16.6); the ASGI test proves the SDK's behaviour upstream of the gateway, and the smoke check above covers the deployed path (Testing Strategy).

*Lambda and Streamable HTTP (F6, H3).* The 2026-07-28 binding is sessionless and lets the server answer every POST with a single JSON object; `json_response=True` + `stateless=True` make both eras request/response, which is exactly Lambda's model. No SSE is ever emitted: every MCP `Route` in the root app accepts **`POST` only**, so Starlette answers `GET`/`DELETE` with 405 + `Allow: POST` — the response the transport spec requires when a server offers no server-initiated stream (the SDK's own 2026 path answers `GET` with 405, but its legacy stateless path would try to open a standalone SSE stream on a `GET` with `Accept: text/event-stream`, which on Lambda would simply hang until the timeout; the `methods=["POST"]` restriction makes that path unreachable). The HTTP API still routes `GET`/`DELETE` to the Lambda so the 405 comes from the server rather than a gateway 404. Because `StreamableHTTPSessionManager.run()` may only be entered once per instance and Mangum runs the ASGI lifespan on every invocation, `handler(event, context)` composes a fresh Starlette app with fresh managers per invocation (D26); the `Server` objects, catalog, verifier and boto3 clients are module-level so warm invocations keep their caches. API Gateway's 30 s integration limit bounds a tool call; the MCP function's own timeout is 29 s and the dispatcher issues `Invoke` (2 s connect + 20 s read, one attempt) only while at least 24 s of invocation time remain, so a backing call always ends — by response or by client-side timeout — inside the function's lifetime and the client always receives an MCP result rather than a gateway 5xx (D32; review-5 M3); every backing REST route is already bounded by the same gateway limit today, and long operations return job identifiers (6.2).

*Root-app composition (H2).* `Server.streamable_http_app()` is not used. Per view `v` with endpoint URL `U_v`: `sm_v = StreamableHTTPSessionManager(app=servers[v], json_response=True, stateless=True, security_settings=TransportSecuritySettings(enable_dns_rebinding_protection=True, allowed_hosts=[MCP_API_HOST], allowed_origins=[]))`; `Route(path_v, endpoint=RequireAuthMiddleware(StreamableHTTPASGIApp(sm_v), [MCP_REQUIRED_SCOPE], build_resource_metadata_url(AnyHttpUrl(U_v))), methods=["POST"])` where `path_v` is `/mcp` or `/mcp/<view>` (exact `Route`, never `Mount`; `POST` only, so `GET`/`DELETE` get Starlette's 405 + `Allow: POST`); `routes += discovery.routes()` — eight unauthenticated `GET` routes rendered by `mcp_portal/discovery.py` (D27): `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource` (root; the canonical `/mcp` PRM), `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/mcp[/<view>]` (six path-suffixed PRMs, `resource` = that endpoint URL, `authorization_servers = [MCP_AS_ISSUER]`, `scopes_supported = [MCP_REQUIRED_SCOPE]`, `bearer_methods_supported = ["header"]`), and `/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` (the RFC 8414 facade, Data Models). The SDK's `create_protected_resource_routes` is **not** used for these: it CORS-wraps (unneeded — no browser client) and, more importantly, a pre-built `AnyHttpUrl("https://host")` carries a trailing slash into the document, which would break Kiro's exact issuer comparison; `discovery.py` emits the environment strings byte-for-byte and a unit test asserts `PRM.authorization_servers[0] == AS.issuer == MCP_AS_ISSUER` with no trailing slash (Requirement 7.5). The challenge URL passed to `RequireAuthMiddleware` still comes from the SDK's `build_resource_metadata_url(AnyHttpUrl(U_v))`, which keeps the path form `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/mcp[/<view>]`. Root middleware, **in this order**: `middleware=[Middleware(AuthFailureAuditMiddleware), Middleware(AuthenticationMiddleware, backend=BearerAuthBackend(VERIFIER)), Middleware(AuthContextMiddleware)]  # Starlette: first entry is outermost — the audit wrapper must enclose the verifier` (review-4 M1). Starlette builds the stack with `for cls, args, kwargs in reversed(middleware): app = cls(app, …)` (`starlette/applications.py:81`, 1.9.0), so the first list entry is the outermost wrapper; `AuthFailureAuditMiddleware` resets `CURRENT_AUTH_FAILURE` at request start and reads it after the response, so it must run **outside** `AuthenticationMiddleware` — the only place `CognitoAccessTokenVerifier.verify_token` sets that variable — or its reset would erase the failure before `RequireAuthMiddleware` (inside the route) answered 401, and the Requirement 4.7 `authentication_failed` entry would never be written. The order is pinned by a test on `build_asgi_app().user_middleware[0].cls`, not only by the audited outcome (Testing Strategy). `resource_server_url` is left `None` on the backend because the six-URL audience rule lives in the verifier. The lifespan is `async with AsyncExitStack() as s: for sm in managers: await s.enter_async_context(sm.run()); yield`.

*Token acquisition, refresh, expiry (concretely).* (1) Kiro POSTs to `/mcp` (or a view URL) without a token and receives **401**. The SDK attaches the `WWW-Authenticate … resource_metadata="https://<id>.execute-api.<region>.amazonaws.com/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/mcp"` challenge, but API Gateway delivers it as `X-Amzn-Remapped-WWW-Authenticate`, so Kiro (per the spec's client rule and its own documentation) constructs the well-known URIs itself. (2) It fetches `GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/mcp` (path-suffixed, first in the spec's fallback order) or `GET /.well-known/oauth-protected-resource` (root, the form Kiro's troubleshooting guide names); both return `{"resource": "https://<id>.execute-api.<region>.amazonaws.com/mcp", "authorization_servers": ["https://<id>.execute-api.<region>.amazonaws.com"], "scopes_supported": ["<canonical>/portal.tools"], "bearer_methods_supported": ["header"]}` (a view URL's suffixed document carries that view's `resource`). (3) It fetches the authorization-server metadata at `https://<id>.execute-api.<region>.amazonaws.com/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server` — the RFC 8414 location for an issuer without a path, the one Kiro documents — and receives the facade document (Data Models) whose `issuer` is the identical string `https://<id>.execute-api.<region>.amazonaws.com` (Kiro's character-for-character check, Requirement 7.5), whose `authorization_endpoint`/`token_endpoint`/`revocation_endpoint` are the existing Cognito hosted-UI URLs, whose `jwks_uri` is Cognito's, and which advertises `code_challenge_methods_supported: ["S256"]` (so the MCP client's PKCE check passes), `response_types_supported: ["code"]`, `grant_types_supported: ["authorization_code", "refresh_token"]`, `token_endpoint_auth_methods_supported: ["none"]`, `scopes_supported: ["<canonical>/portal.tools"]`. (4) It opens the browser at Cognito's `/oauth2/authorize?response_type=code&client_id=<mcp client>&redirect_uri=http://localhost:7899/oauth/callback&scope=<canonical>/portal.tools&resource=<endpoint URL>&code_challenge=…&code_challenge_method=S256`; the user signs in with the existing portal account (SRP or SAML, exactly as the web UI). (5) Kiro exchanges the code at Cognito's `/oauth2/token` (public client, PKCE verifier) and receives an **access token** (1 h) and a **refresh token** (30 d), both issued by Cognito — the facade issues nothing and sees no credential. (6) Every MCP request carries `Authorization: Bearer <access token>`; at expiry Kiro uses the refresh grant, whose access token carries the same `aud`. (7) When an administrator disables the account in the User Manager, Cognito refuses the refresh grant immediately; the outstanding access token dies within its remaining ≤ 1 h lifetime — the same bound the web UI has (2.8). (8) The server answers an expired or invalid token with 401 and the client refreshes or re-authenticates (2.6; Kiro "automatically triggers a new browser-based authentication flow" when no refresh token is available). Example `mcp.json` entries are in "Client configuration".

*Why a facade and not Cognito's own discovery document (review-2 M1).* The previous revision advertised the Cognito issuer and relied on clients reaching Cognito's OpenID Connect Discovery document through the spec's third fallback. Two documented facts contradict that path: Kiro's configuration page describes RFC 8414 (`/.well-known/oauth-authorization-server`) as the required location and never mentions OpenID Connect Discovery, and both of the pool's RFC 8414 locations return HTTP 400 (live probe); independently, Cognito's OIDC document lacks `code_challenge_methods_supported`, which the MCP security-considerations page says a client MUST treat as "no PKCE" and refuse. A static RFC 8414 document served by the MCP Lambda removes both blockers. What it changes and does not change: the `issuer` string is the MCP API origin (so the PRM's `authorization_servers[0]` and the document's `issuer` are one environment variable, `MCP_AS_ISSUER`); the endpoints are Cognito's hosted UI unchanged; the token verifier is unchanged and still requires `iss == COGNITO_ISSUER` (the verifier, not the PRM, owns that check); no credential, code or token passes through the facade. **Requirement 2.4 reading:** "publish Protected_Resource_Metadata naming the User_Pool as the authorization server" is satisfied in substance — the facade *is* the User_Pool's authorization and token endpoints and JWKS, published under a metadata document the pool itself does not publish at the location clients need — and the owner is asked to confirm this reading (Phase 1 sign-off). Direct advertisement of the Cognito issuer remains documented in the ADMIN_GUIDE as an alternative to try only if a future Kiro release documents OIDC discovery and Cognito adds the PKCE field; it is not the MVP path.

*Cognito mapping of the MCP authorization model.* Resource server identifier = canonical URL (URL-format identifiers are documented), scope name `portal.tools` (full scope string `<canonical>/portal.tools`), app client `dda-portal-mcp-client`: `generateSecret: false`, `oAuth.flows.authorizationCodeGrant`, `scopes: [resourceServer scope]` only (no `openid`, so no ID token is issued to MCP clients), callback URLs `http://localhost:7899/oauth/callback` plus any extra from CDK context `mcpRedirectUris` (each must be `http://localhost[:port]/path` or `https://…` — Cognito accepts plain HTTP for `localhost` only, Research baseline), `preventUserExistenceErrors: true`, same identity providers as the web client (Cognito, plus the SAML provider when `ssoEnabled`), and **`ExplicitAuthFlows = ['ALLOW_REFRESH_TOKEN_AUTH']` exactly** (review-3 H1): written in CDK as `authFlows: { userSrp: false }` with a comment, because `configureAuthFlows` omits the property for `{}` and Cognito then defaults to refresh **plus** `ALLOW_USER_SRP_AUTH` and `ALLOW_CUSTOM_AUTH`, which would let a password-holding script mint MCP access tokens through `InitiateAuth` without the hosted UI or PKCE; with the refresh flow alone, the authorization-code grant and the refresh grant are the only token paths, and `mcp-stack.test.ts` asserts the emitted value (Testing Strategy). Cognito accepting the refresh flow as the sole explicit flow on a code-grant client is expected (the hosted-UI grants are governed by `AllowedOAuthFlows`, not `ExplicitAuthFlows`) and is confirmed on the deployed stack with `aws cognito-idp describe-user-pool-client` as part of the Phase-1 smoke evidence. Both resources are created **in `EdgeCVPortalMcpStack`'s own scope** (`new cognito.UserPoolResourceServer(this, …)`, `new cognito.UserPoolClient(this, …)`) on the pool passed in from `AuthStack`; `userPool.addResourceServer()` is deliberately not used because it would place the resource server inside `AuthStack` and create a cross-stack cycle with the API id. Pre-registration (`oauth.clientId` in `mcp.json`) is the registration mechanism: the pool's discovery document advertises neither a `registration_endpoint` nor `client_id_metadata_document_supported` (live probe 2026-10-18).

*Known client-side behaviours to validate first (MVP exit criteria).* The two former items about PKCE metadata and the OIDC fallback are resolved by design (the facade, above) rather than left to client behaviour. What remains to observe on a deployed stack: (i) the **header smoke check** (which header name carries the challenge through API Gateway — recorded, not relied upon); (ii) Kiro must request **only** the configured `oauthScopes` (Cognito rejects unknown scopes such as `offline_access` with `invalid_scope`); if Kiro appends defaults, the documented fallback is `"oauthScopes": []`, which makes Cognito issue all scopes the client allows — exactly the one `portal.tools` scope; (iii) Cognito stamping `aud` for the five view URLs is **documented** ("a URL of your choosing"), so it is verified, not assumed; (iv) Kiro's refresh grant against Cognito preserves `aud` on the refreshed access token (expected; if not, the `client_id`+scope fallback of D3 still admits the token and the observation is recorded); (v) **Kiro IDE and Kiro CLI bind the configured `redirectUri` literally — host and port — and Cognito matches callback URLs exactly** (review-3 N9): the registered default is `http://localhost:7899/oauth/callback` and `mcp.json` must carry that same string; Kiro's documentation example uses `http://127.0.0.1:8080/oauth/callback`, but Cognito accepts plain HTTP only for the host `localhost` (Research baseline), so `127.0.0.1` is **not** an option on this pool — if a Kiro build insists on another port, `mcpRedirectUris` registers it (the CDK validator rejects any plain-HTTP host other than `localhost` at synth with a message pointing at this rule); (vi) the deployed MCP app client reports `ExplicitAuthFlows: ['ALLOW_REFRESH_TOKEN_AUTH']` (`describe-user-pool-client`, recorded with the smoke evidence); (vii) **Kiro accepts the root PRM whose `resource` is `<canonical>/mcp`** (review-4 N1): RFC 9728 §3.3 requires the `resource` value to equal the identifier the well-known suffix was inserted into, which for the root location is the origin, not `…/mcp`; the official SDK client accepts a PRM `resource` that is a path-prefix parent of the server URL (`mcp/client/auth/oauth2.py:580–592` → `mcp/shared/auth_utils.py:31–65 check_resource_allowed`), Kiro's rule is undocumented (kiro.dev MCP configuration page, re-read 2026-10-18: the troubleshooting entry fetches the root PRM and reads `authorization_servers[0]` only) — if Kiro rejects it, the fallback is the root document carrying `resource: MCP_AS_ISSUER` (the origin) with the origin added to `MCP_ALLOWED_AUDIENCES` as a seventh accepted value (Data Models); (viii) **Kiro IDE and CLI send no `Origin` header on MCP requests** (review-5 N5) — both servers answer 403 to any `Origin` (D14), which is right for Node-side HTTP clients but would fail every request if a Kiro build issued them from an Electron renderer (`Origin: vscode-webview://…` or `null`); recorded during the smoke check from the HTTP API access log / the Lambda's transport-security WARNING count and, on the edge, the proxy's `api.access` 403 counter; **fallback, empty by default:** `MCP_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` (portal Lambda environment from CDK context `mcpAllowedOrigins`, CSV → `TransportSecuritySettings.allowed_origins`) and `McpAllowedOrigins` (edge component-configuration key, CSV, absent ⇒ empty → the proxy's allow-list and the child's `--allowed-origins`); with a non-empty list the rule becomes "an `Origin` outside the list → 403", `Origin` is still never forwarded to the child, and the SDK's loopback defaults are still never used.

### Decision point 3 — Edge hosting, transport, and Python/package constraints

**Recommendation: in-image virtualenv + supervised loopback child process + `/mcp` reverse-proxy route on the existing Backend_API listener (D11–D16, D20).**

*Rejected: mounting the SDK in the Backend_API process.* It would force `pydantic 2.6.4 → ≥2.12`, `jsonschema 4.17.3 → ≥4.20` (adds native `rpds-py`), `uvicorn 0.23.2 → ≥0.31.1` and `fastapi` to a matching line on JP5/JP7/amd64 — a runtime change that applies even with MCP disabled, violating C7's "exactly as today" — and `uvicorn` would move on **every** image including JP6 (the vLLM layer never bumped it). Aligning the fleet's pins is a separate decision with its own on-device verification (Backlog 6).

*Rejected: a sidecar container.* It needs a third `docker save` image in `build-custom.sh`, a new compose service (gate-tracked) in each profile, `docker load` lines and lifecycle changes in all seven recipe variants, a second health gate, and a second LAN-exposed port under `network_mode: host`.

*Rejected: a stdio process on the device.* Reachable only over SSH, still needs the SDK installed, and gives no HMI/portal-visible status.

*Chosen.* Each backend Dockerfile (`Dockerfile`, `Dockerfile.jp5`, `Dockerfile.jp6`, `Dockerfile.jp7`, `Dockerfile.x86_64_nvidia`) gains **two** additions (H1). **(a) The venv block** — `COPY mcp_edge/requirements.lock.txt ./mcp_edge/requirements.lock.txt` followed by `RUN python3 -m venv /opt/dda/mcp-venv && /opt/dda/mcp-venv/bin/pip install --no-cache-dir --only-binary :all: -r ./mcp_edge/requirements.lock.txt && /opt/dda/mcp-venv/bin/python -c "import mcp, mcp.server; print('mcp venv OK')"` — is inserted **directly after the step that installs the backend requirements, which differs per file (review-2 M3):** `Dockerfile.jp5` after line 114 (`RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r ./requirements.txt`), `Dockerfile.jp6` after line 163 (same), `Dockerfile.jp7` after line 245 (same), `Dockerfile` after line 44 (`RUN ./edge_ml1_p_camera_management/prereqs_install.sh`, whose line 28 runs `python3 -m pip install -r ./requirements.txt`), `Dockerfile.x86_64_nvidia` after line 60 (the same `RUN ./edge_ml1_p_camera_management/prereqs_install.sh`). At each anchor `python3` already resolves to the backend interpreter — `update-alternatives` at `Dockerfile:13–14` and `Dockerfile.x86_64_nvidia:32–33` (→ `/usr/local/bin/python3.11`), `Dockerfile.jp5:93–94` (→ `/usr/local/bin/python${PYTHON_VERSION}`, 3.11), `Dockerfile.jp6:119–120` and `Dockerfile.jp7:195–196` (→ `/usr/bin/python${PYTHON_VERSION}`, 3.10 and 3.11) — so `python3 -m venv` creates a 3.11 / 3.11 / 3.11 / 3.10 / 3.11 venv on `Dockerfile` / `.jp5` / `.jp7` / `.jp6` / `.x86_64_nvidia` respectively. **(b) The package copy** — `COPY mcp_edge ./mcp_edge` directly after `COPY user_accounts_sync ./user_accounts_sync` (`Dockerfile:122`, `Dockerfile.jp5:230`, `Dockerfile.jp6:442`, `Dockerfile.jp7:586`, `Dockerfile.x86_64_nvidia:144`); no Dockerfile has a wholesale `COPY . .`, so without this line both the supervisor import in the backend and `-m mcp_edge.child` in the venv fail with `ModuleNotFoundError`. The venv interpreter is the image's `python3`: 3.11 on JP5 (from source, `--with-ensurepip=install`), 3.10 on JP6 (distro + `python3.10-venv`, line 54), 3.11 on JP7 (distro + `-venv`, line 139), and 3.11.9 **built from source** on the generic `Dockerfile` and `Dockerfile.x86_64_nvidia`, where `python3 -m venv` works because the `ensurepip` stdlib module ships its bundled wheels (N3). `mcp` 2.3.0 requires ≥ 3.10, so both interpreters are supported. **Pins (D20):** the full set resolved on 2026-10-18 under Python 3.10 is listed in the Research baseline; the two version-sensitive pins are `rpds-py==0.30.0` (last cp310 release; `jsonschema>=4.20` → `referencing` → `rpds-py`) and `starlette==1.7.0` (last release supporting 3.10); everything else is the current release and identical on both interpreters; `exceptiongroup==1.3.1; python_version < "3.11"` is the only environment-marker line. The implementation task re-resolves at lock time with `uv pip compile --python-version 3.10 --python-platform aarch64-manylinux_2_35`, installs the same lock under 3.11 (aarch64 and x86_64) with `--only-binary :all:` to prove wheel availability, and records the cp310/cp311 aarch64 and cp311 x86_64 wheel names for `rpds-py`, `pydantic-core`, `cryptography`, `cffi` in the task evidence (13.5). The venv adds roughly 40 MB to each image; no runtime `pip` ever runs.
